## CHAPTER XXXII

## Yoga and Dharmaśāstra

[[P1385]]Sāṅkhya and Yoga often go together in the Upaniṣads [[28|as]] well as in the Mahābhārata, the [[Bhagavadgitā|Bhagavadgītā]] and the Purāṇas and their relationship to one another is the same in all these. In [[Sv.|Śv.]] Up. VI. 13, Vanaparva 2. 15, [[śāntiparva|Śāntiparva]][^2270] [[228, 28,|228. 28,]] [[289.1,|289. 1,]] 306. 65, [[308, 25,|308. 25,]] 326. 100, 336. 69, Anuśāsana 14. 323, [[Bhagavad gitā|Bhagavadgītā]] V. [[4-5,|4–5,]] Padma-[[purāpa|purāṇa]] ([[Pātālakbanda,|Pātālakhaṇḍa,]] 85. 11 ff), the two are mentioned [[together, |together.]]

Though [[Saṅkhya|Sāṅkhya]] greatly influenced all works dealing with the evolution of the world in its various [[aspecte,|aspects,]] it did not enjoy that great esteem among all sorts of people in India that the Yoga system enjoyed and still enjoys. The word yoga is derived from the root 'yuj' to join or to unite (of rudhādi class). The germs of yoga can be traced back to the [[Rgveda.|Ṛgveda.]] [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] V. 81. 1 [[( a |(a ]]verse in praise of [[Savitp )|Savitṛ)]] reads 'wise men, priests and sacrificers concentrate their minds and join their prayers to the wise, the great (Savitṛ), who knows all (prayers)'. Another [[Vedic -271|Vedic]][^2271] verse also speaks of concentrating the mind. The word 'yoga' occurs frequently in the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] in several shades of meaning. [[Sāyana|Sāyaṇa]] in many passages takes 'yoga' to mean 'acquiring what is not already possessed' (as in [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] I. 5. 3). In [[Rg,|Ṛg.]] I. 18. 7 God Sadasaspati (Agni) is said to pervade the prayers (or thoughts) of sacrificers. In [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] I. 34. 9 it means 'yoking' ([['kada|('kadā]] yogo vājino [[rāsabhagya|rāsabhasya]] yena yajñam [[Bāsatyo payāthaḥ'.|Nāsatyopayāthaḥ').]] The word 'yoga' is often used with the word kṣema (separately as in [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] VII. 54. 3, VII. 86. 8) or as a compound

[[P1386]](as in [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] X. 166. 5 'Yogakṣemam va [[adayaham bbūyāsam uttamal').|ādāyāhaṁ bhūyāsamuttamah').]] There is a great distance or gap between the meaning of the word yoga in the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] (even in the verses about concentrating the mind) and its meaning in some of the Upaniṣads and in classical Sanskrit. In [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] X. 136. [[2-3 thero|2–3 there]] is [[mention 3272|mention]][^2272] of munis, [[eons|sons]] of [[Vātarajada,|Vātaraśana,]] who wore dirty and tawny garments and who say in verse 3 'we being in [[oostasy|ecstasy]] (or wild bliss) owing to [[cur|our]] way of life as munis resort to winds; mortals! you look on our bodies only'. This shows that even in those ancient times, some people practised tapas, did not care what clothes they wore and thought they (i.e. their souls) would be merged in the wind (that is, the souls are formless and could not be seen). In [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] VIII. 17. 14 Indra is said to be a friend of munis and muni is also a friend of every god in [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] X. 136. 4. But as to yatis the position was rather different. The word [[yali|yati]] occurs several times in the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] but in most cases that word has no certain connection with the meaning of [['ascetio'.|'ascetic'.]] In [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] VIII. 3. 9 'by which (says [[brabma|brahmā]] priest) wealth was given to [[Börgu|Bhṛgu]] from the yatis and by which you helped (or protected) [[Praskanva';|Praskaṇva';]] here Indra [[sooms|seems]] to be opposed to yatis. In [[Rg. VIII, 6. 18|Ṛg. VIII. 6. 18]] the poet says 'O valiant Indra! listen to my prayer alone from among the yatis and Bhṛgus that praised [[thee |thee'.]] Here Sāyaṇa explains 'yatayaḥ' as [['Angirasah'.|'Aṅgirasaḥ'.]] In any case 'yatis' are here shown to be devotees of Indra. But in other [[Sarbitas|Saṁhitās]] it is said that Indra threw 'yatis' to the wolves or [[hyenas, 1773|hyenas.]][^2273] Later on, however, the meaning of yati seems to have changed. In those Samhitā passages 'yatis' appear to be persons hostile or alien to the Vedic rites, but what they did to deserve

[[P1387]]slaughter by Indra is not clear. In Atharvaveda II. 5. 3 Indra is said to have killed Vṛtra as he did in the case of yatis. Some of the Upaniṣads, however, show that 'yati' was a person who had given up worldly affairs, practised yoga and endeavoured after the knowledge of Ātman and realized Brahma; vide Muṇḍaka Up. III. 1. 5 (yaṁ paśyanti yatayaḥ kṣīṇadoṣāḥ) and III. 2. 6 (sannyāsayogād-yatayaḥ śuddhasattvāḥ). Some like Haüer in 'Die [[Anfrange|Anfänge]] der Yoga-praxis', 1922 pp. 11 ff) hold that the Vrātyas of Atharvaveda XV were ecstatics of the kṣatriya class and forerunners of Yogins.

The word 'yoga' occurs in some of the Upaniṣads in the same sense in which it is employed in the Yogasūtra. In Kaṭha Up. II. [[1%,|12,]] it is said[^2274]] 'the wise man reflecting on God by means of yoga, by concentrating the mind on the inner spirit becomes free from joy and grief' ([[adhyātmayogadhigamona|adhyātmayogādhigamena]]). The same Upaniṣad says that the state described in VI. 11 is regarded as Yoga because therein the organs (and the mind and buddhi) are firmly held under control. [[Ia|In]] Kaṭha Up. [[VL.|VI.]] 18 it is said that Naciketas having understood the vidyā and the whole procedure of Yoga propounded by Yama attained (the realization of) brahma. The word 'Yoga' occurs in the Tai. Up. II. 4 (in speaking about vijñānamaya ātman, yoga is said to be its ātman, the exact sense being doubtful), and in the Śv. Up. II. 11 and VI. 13. [[Praśna (5.5-6)|Praśna (5. 5–6)]] speaks of the three mātrās (a, [[u,m|u, m)]] of Om. In Śv. Up. [[1. 3 Occurs|I. 3 occurs]] the word 'dhyānayoga'. The Śv. Up. (II. 8–13) refers to āsana and [[prāpāyāma|prāṇāyāma]] and sets out the first signs of the successful [[practics|practice]] of Yoga. The Chān. Up. VIII. 15 appears to refer to pratyāhāra (though the technical word is not [[used )|used)]] in [['atmani|'ātmani]] sarvendriyāṇi [[pratiṣthāpya'(having|pratiṣṭhāpya' (having]] brought all organs of sense to rest in the [[atman).'|ātman).']] The [[Bļ.|Bṛ.]] Up. ([[1.5.23|I. 5. 23]]) appears to refer to prāṇāyāma in 'he should observe the one vow, viz. inhalation and [[exhala tion|exhalation]] ([[tasmad-ekameva|tasmād-ekameva]] vratam [[carot prāṇyao-oaiva apanyacca).|caret prāṇyāc-caivāpānyācca)'.]]

The idea appears to be that yoga had been fully developed at the time of the Katha Up, but that Upanimd did not set it out in detail. It may be further noted that the words gat ragt refer to Brahmavidya and that yoga-vidhi is separately raentioned (probably as a means to brahma. realization).|तां योगमिति मन्यन्ते स्थिरामिन्द्रियधारणाम् । कठ० VI. 11; मृत्युप्रोक्तां नचिकेतोऽथ लब्ध्वा विद्यामेतां योगविधिं च कृत्स्नम् । ब्रह्मप्राप्तो विरजोऽभूद्विमृत्युरन्योप्येवं यो विदध्यात्ममेव ॥ कठ. VI. 18. The important words in this last are कृत्स्नं योगविधिम्. The idea appears to be that yoga had been fully developed at the time of the Kaṭha Up. but that Upaniṣad did not set it out in detail. It may be further noted that the words एतां विद्यां refer to Brahmavidyā and that yoga-vidhi is separately mentioned (probably as a means to brahma-realization).]]

[[P1388]]The [[Vedantasūtra|Vedāntasūtra]] (II. 1. 3 'etena yogaḥ [[pratyuktah)|pratyuktaḥ')]] states that Yoga is refuted by the reasoning adopted for the refutation of the Sāṅkhya system. The position of [[Saskarāoārga|Śaṅkarācārya]] as to both [[Sāokhya|Sāṅkhya]] and Yoga has been stated above (pp. 1352 and 1361 n. 2208 and 2221). He points out in the pūrvapakṣa that [[Voda 2375|Veda]][^2275] [[proscribes|prescribes]] Yoga as a means of achieving correct knowledge and refers to Bṛ. Up. II. 4. 5. He further points out that in the Śv. Up. Yoga is expounded in great detail after first mentioning the proper posture (for the practice of Yoga) in the words 'having held the body [[straigbt|straight]] but raised high in three places viz. chest, neck and head' (Śv. Up. II. 8). From the words of Śaṅkarācārya that in Yogaśāstra also Yoga is held to be a means of the realisation of right knowledge, it follows that [[be|he]] had a yogaśāstra before him in which occurred the words 'atha...[[yogah,'|yogaḥ',]] but as he does not [[einploy|employ]] the word Yogasūtra he probably does not refer to a sūtra work. If a conjecture may be hazarded, it is possible that the Yogaśāstra meant by [[Śhaṅkara|Śaṅkara]] is a work like the Yogaśāstra ascribed to Yājñavalkya in Yāj. [[Smrti|Smṛti]] III. 110 ([[Yogaśāstram ca mat-proktam &c.).|Yogaśāstraṁ ca mat-proktaṁ &c.).]] [[Śhaṅkaracārya|Śaṅkarācārya]] admits on V. S. [[]], 1, 3|II. 1. 3]] that a part of the yoga is acceptable to him but other parts conflict with the Veda. Muṇḍaka Up. ([[II 2.6|II. 2. 6]]) enjoins [[samūdhi|samādhi]] in the words 'om-iti dhyāyatha ātmānam' as Śaṅkarācārya states on V. S. II. 3. 39 [[(samadhyabhāvāc-ca).|(samādhyabhāvāc-ca).]] In the [[Upanisada|Upaniṣads]] 'Muni' and 'Yati' have come to [[denote 2276|denote]][^2276] the same meaning, [[o g.|e.g.]] Bṛ. Up. IV. 4. 22 states 'one becomes muni by realizing this very self,'

[[P1389]]while the Muṇḍaka (III. 1. [[5) 2777|5)]][^2277] states 'this self is to be attained by Truth, by tapas, by right knowledge and by sexual purity at all times: that self resides inside the body (like a light), is pure, whom spotless ascetics realize.' The [[Kathopaniṣad|Kaṭhopaniṣad]] (III. [[13),|13)]] provides that the wise man should restrain speech in mind ([[ speech| (speech]] and mind, as the text stands), he should keep it within the self which is knowledge, he should keep down knowledge within the self that [[is,|is]] the great, and [[he,|he]] should keep that (the great) within the self which is quiet. The [[Upaniṣada|Upaniṣads]] thus not only employ the word 'Yoga' but provide some of the stages of yoga and its technique for [[realiz ing|realizing]] the Supreme Spirit. About twenty Yoga Upaniṣads are published at Adyar, edited by A. Mahadev Sastri (1920), but as their chronology is most uncertain and as most of them appear to the present writer to be later than the Mahābhārata, Manu and probably the Yogasūtra, they are passed over here and are referred to in this work sparingly.[^2278]

Pāṇini provides for the formation of the words yama and niyama (two [[angas)|aṅgas)]] of Yoga and the word 'Yogin' appears to have been derived by him from the root 'yuj' with the affix [['ghinun'(i.|'ghinun' (i.]] e. in) in the sense of [[tūcchīlya|tācchīlya]] (being [[babituated|habituated]] to) according to Pāṇ. III. 2. 142 (which is a very long sūtra).[^2279]]

III. 13. शङ्कराचार्य on वे. सू. I. 4. 1 explains this at length 'वाचं मनसि संयच्छेत् वागादिबाह्येन्द्रियव्यापारमुत्सृज्य मनोमात्रेणावतिष्ठेत'. He takes मनसी as an ārṣaprayoga equal to 'मनसि'.]]

[^2278]: That the yoga Upaniṣads are late productions may be briefly indicated here. Verses 10–14 of the [[trea|गोरक्षशतक]] (dealing with the [[adhāra|ādhāra]] and [[svadhisthāna|svādhiṣṭhāna]] cakras) are found in the [[race|ध्यानबिन्दु]] (verses [[,| ]]43–47) and [[la|in]] the [[grap|योगचूडामणि]] (verses 4–9) with slight variations. Vide pp. 1060–1062 [[aod|and]] notes 1715–1717 above for the six chakras and the [[pādis.|nāḍīs.]] In the description of prāṇāyāma the [[sandilya|Śāṇḍilya]] [[Upanisad|Upaniṣad]] quotes certain verses with the words तदेते श्लोका भवन्ति, some of which occur in the गोरक्षशतक. One cannot say definitely that the [[sandilya|Śāṇḍilya]] borrows from G. S., but this is possible. All the ancient and medieval works on the several branches of yoga are not yet available, and therefore one cannot preclude the [[possibilsty|possibility]] that [[sāndilya|Śāṇḍilya]] and other yoga [[upanigads|upaniṣads]] and [[three|those]] borrow from earlier sources not yet discovered.

[^2279]: [[यमः समुपनिविषु च । पा. III. 3. 63; पषु अनुपसर्गे च यमेर्अप् वा !... नियमः नियामः । यमः यामः । सि. कौ. याम meads प्रहर (watch, 1/8 part of the whole day), while यम means 'rostraint' 'यम्पते चित्तं येने'. On पा. III. 2. 142 the काशिका remarks 'युज समाधौ दिवादिः । युजिर् योगे रुधादिः । दयोरपि ग्रहणम्'.|यमः समुपनिविषु च । पा. III. 3. 63; एषु अनुपसर्गे च यमेर्अप् वा !... नियमः नियामः । यमः यामः । सि. कौ. याम means प्रहर (watch, 1/8 part of the whole day), while यम means 'restraint' 'यम्यते चित्तं येन'. On पा. III. 2. 142 the काशिका remarks 'युज समाधौ दिवादिः । युजिर् योगे रुधादिः । द्वयोरपि ग्रहणम्'.]]

[[P1390]]The [[Apastambadharmasūtra|Āpastambadharmasūtra]] (I. 8. 23. 3–6) quotes a verse 'The total destruction of doṣas (taints) is due to Yoga in this [[life ;|life;]] a wise man after having expelled all doṣas that cause harm to all beings reaches peace ([[moksa|mokṣa]])' and then it sets out fifteen doṣas such as anger, ecstatic joy, greed, hypocrisy, the destruction of which is due to Yoga and enumerates the qualities that are opposites of the doṣas. This shows that long before the 4th or 5th century B. C. Yoga as a discipline of the mind had been well developed.

The V. S. II. 1. 3 indicates that the Sūtrakāra had before him a body of Yoga doctrines, some of which were the same as those of the Sāṅkhya and he also knew Samādhi (V. S. II. 3. 39). Again, the V. S. mentions (in IV. 2. 21) Yogins and distinguishes Sāṅkhya and Yoga as smārta (and not śrauta). Śaṅkarācārya on V. S. I. 3. 33 quotes the extant Yogasūtra II. 44 (svādhyāyādiṣṭa-devatā-samprayogaḥ) and [[on 2280|on]][^2280] V. S. II. 4. 12 he appears to be willing to admit that the Yoga-sūtra preceded V. S. and quotes Yogasūtra I. 6 in the 2nd interpretation of that sūtra.

The important question is whether the author of the V. S. refers to the extant Yogasūtra. The present author holds for various reasons that the V. S. does not refer to the present Yoga-sūtra, but to the Yoga doctrines that had been developed even before the Kaṭha, Muṇḍaka, Śvetāśvatara and other Upaniṣads.

In the Śāntiparva it is stated that the [[propounder 2281|propounder]][^2281] of Sāṅkhya was paramarṣi (highest sage) Kapila, [[Hiranyagarbba|Hiraṇyagarbha]]

[^2280]: [[पञ्चवृत्तिर्मनोवव्यपदिश्यते । वे. . II. 4. 12; शांकरभाष्य इतश्चास्ति मुख्यस्य माणस्य वैशेषिकं कार्य यत्कारणं पञ्चवृत्तिरयं व्यपदिश्यते श्रुतिषु माणोऽपानो व्यान उदानः समानः-इति (बृह. उप. I. 5. 3) । ...एवं तर्हि परमतममतिषिद्धमनुमतं भवतीति न्यायाविहापि योगशास्त्रप्रसिद्धा मनसः पञ्च वृत्तयः परिगृह्यन्ते-प्रमाणविपर्ययविकल्पनिद्रा. Fatat ATA I. It would be noticed that Sarkarācārya first gave one explan. ation of the word 'manovat' in the V. 8. and then he gave another by way of concession.|पञ्चवृत्तिर्मनोवद्व्यपदिश्यते । वे. सू. II. 4. 12; शांकरभाष्य 'इतश्चास्ति मुख्यस्य प्राणस्य वैशेषिकं कार्यं यत्कारणं पञ्चवृत्तिरयं व्यपदिश्यते श्रुतिषु प्राणोऽपानो व्यान उदानः समानः-इति (बृह. उप. I. 5. 3) । ...एवं तर्हि परमतमप्रतिषिद्धमनुमतं भवतीति न्यायादिहापि योगशास्त्रप्रसिद्धा मनसः पञ्च वृत्तयः परिगृह्यन्ते-प्रमाणविपर्ययविकल्पनिद्रास्मृतयो नाम ।. It would be noticed that Śaṅkarācārya first gave one explanation of the word 'manovat' in the V. S. and then he gave another by way of concession.]]

[^2281]: [[सांख्यं योगं...नाना मतानि वै ॥ सांख्यस्य वक्ता कपिलः परमर्षिः स उच्यते । हिरण्यगर्भो योगस्य वेत्ता (v. l. वक्ता) नान्यः पुरातनः ॥ अपान्तरतमाश्चैव वेदाचार्यः स उच्यते । प्राचीनगर्भ तमृषिं प्रवदन्तीह केचन ॥ शान्ति 337. 59–61 = चित्र० ed. 349. 64-66. For the first verse, vide above p. 954 n 1544 and also 'सांख्यं योगः पञ्चरात्रं वेदारण्यकमेव च । ज्ञानान्येतानि बार्थे लोकेषु प्रचरन्ति हि ॥ शान्ति 337. 1.; एवमेकं सांख्ययोगं वेदारण्यकमेव च । परस्पराङ्गन्येतानि पञ्चरात्रं च कथ्यते । एष एकान्तिनां धर्मो नारायणपरात्मकः ॥ शान्ति 336. 76 ( 348. 81-82 Ch. ed.). वेदारण्यक probably refers to upaniṣads like the Brhadāraṇyaka and Chandogya, which contain passages about 'nididhyāsa' and the non-difference of jiva and brahma such as tat-tvam-asi'. A paramarși is defined in the argger as 'निवृत्तिसमकालं तु बुद्ध्याऽव्यक्तमृषिः स्वयम् । परं हि ऋषते यस्मात्परमर्षिस्ततः स्मृतः । 59. 80 (vide same verse in Brahmāṇḍa III. 32. 86).|सांख्यं योगं...नाना मतानि वै ॥ सांख्यस्य वक्ता कपिलः परमर्षिः स उच्यते । हिरण्यगर्भो योगस्य वेत्ता (v. l. वक्ता) नान्यः पुरातनः ॥ अपान्तरतमाश्चैव वेदाचार्यः स उच्यते । प्राचीनगर्भं तमृषिं प्रवदन्तीह केचन ॥ शान्ति 337. 59–61 = चित्र० ed. 349. 64–66. For the first verse, vide above p. 954 n. 1544 and also 'सांख्यं योगः पञ्चरात्रं वेदारण्यकमेव च । ज्ञानान्येतानि बह्वर्थे लोकेषु प्रचरन्ति हि ॥ शान्ति 337. 1.; एवमेकं सांख्ययोगं वेदारण्यकमेव च । परस्पराङ्गन्येतानि पञ्चरात्रं च कथ्यते । एष एकान्तिनां धर्मो नारायणपरात्मकः ॥ शान्ति 336. 76 (= 348. 81–82 Ch. ed.). वेदारण्यक probably refers to upaniṣads like the Bṛhadāraṇyaka and Chāndogya, which contain passages about 'nididhyāsa' and the non-difference of jīva and brahma such as 'tat-tvam-asi'. A paramarṣi is defined in the वायुपुराण as 'निवृत्तिसमकालं तु बुद्ध्याऽव्यक्तमृषिः स्वयम् । परं हि ऋषते यस्मात्परमर्षिस्ततः स्मृतः । 59. 80 (vide same verse in Brahmāṇḍa III. 32. 86).]]

[[P1391]]was the ancient knower of Yoga, no one else (knew it); Apāntaratamas was Vedācārya whom some called the sage [[Prāclnagarbha.|Prācīnagarbha.]] In the previous chapter it is stated that Sāṅkhya, Yoga, Vedāraṇyaka and Pañcarātra are one and are [[angas|aṅgas]] of each other. In Śānti. 326. 65 Hiraṇyagarbha is again connected with Yogaśāstra. Vide p. 1371 above. In the [[Anuśāsanaparva 2282|Anuśāsanaparva]][^2282] Sanatkumāra appears to be connected with Yoga as the founder, just as Kapila was the founder of Sāṅkhya. In the Ahirbudhnya Saṁhitā (XII. [[32-33|32–33]]) it is stated that Hiraṇyagarbha first propounded two Yogasaṁhitās, one was called 'Nirodhayoga' and the other was called 'Karmayoga', the first of which was again divided twelve-fold. The [[Bhāmat! 2283|Bhāmatī]][^2283] on V. S. II. 1. 3 states that that sūtra does not totally negative the authoritativeness of the Yogaśāstra of Hiraṇyagarbha and Patañjali. The Viṣṇu-[[purāpa 2284|purāṇa]][^2284] appears to quote two verses from Hiraṇyagarbha (vide note below). Vācaspati in his commentary on Yogasūtra I. 1 states that Yogi-Yājñavalkya mentions that Hiraṇyagarbha was the propounder of Yoga. Vācaspati regards the Yogasūtra of Patañjali as later than [[Yoga-Yājñavalkya-smrti.|Yoga-Yājñavalkya-smṛti.]] Therefore, it is almost certain that the V. S. refutes the doctrines of that Yoga system that was known to the Śāntiparva.

In Śalyaparva (chap. 50) a story is told about bhikṣu [[Jaiglṣavya|Jaigīṣavya]] who was a great Yogin and of 'Asita Devala', a householder staying on the holy Sārasvata-tīrtha.

In the Śāntiparva (cr. ed. chap. 222 = Ch. ed. chap. 229) there is a long dialogue between Jaigīṣavya and Asita about Yoga, one verse [[(14) 2285|(14)]][^2285] from which may be cited

I. 2. The half verse हिरण्य...पुरातनः is बृहद्योगियाज्ञवल्क्य XII. 5 (Lonavla); संभावना परां हानि योगः कुरुते यतः । जनेनाव मतो योगी योगसिद्धिं च विन्दति ॥ तस्माच्चरेत वै योगी सतां धर्ममदूषयन् । जना यथावम न्येयन् गच्छेयुर्नैव सङ्गतिम् ॥ हिरण्यगर्भश्चैवं विचिन्त्याथ महामतिः । आत्मानं दर्शयामास जनोन्मत्ताकृतिं जने ॥ विष्णु II. 13. 42-44.|ननु हिरण्यगर्भो योगस्य वक्ता नान्यः पुरातनः-इति योगियाज्ञवल्क्यस्मृतेः कथं पतञ्जलेर्योगशास्त्रकर्तृत्वमित्याशङ्कय सूत्रकारेणानुशासनमित्युक्तम् । शिष्टस्यानुशासनं न तु शासनमित्यर्थः । वाचस्पति on यो. I. 2. The half verse हिरण्य...पुरातनः is बृहद्योगियाज्ञवल्क्य XII. 5 (Lonavla); संभावना परां हानिं योगर्द्धेः कुरुते यतः । जनेनावमतो योगी योगसिद्धिं च विन्दति ॥ तस्माच्चरेत वै योगी सतां धर्ममदूषयन् । जना यथावमन्येरन् गच्छेयुर्नैव सङ्गतिम् ॥ हिरण्यगर्भश्चैवं विचिन्त्याथ महामतिः । आत्मानं दर्शयामास जनोन्मत्ताकृतिं जने ॥ विष्णु II. 13. 42–44.]]

[^2285]: निन्दामशंसैश्चात्यर्थं न वदन्ति परस्य ये । न च निन्दाप्रशंसाभ्यां विक्रियन्ते कदाचन ॥ शान्ति 229. 14 (Ch. ed.).

[[P1392]]here 'Yogins do not talk much by way of censure and praise of others and their minds are never affected by the praise and censure of them indulged in by others.' In that chap. [[Jaigtṣavya|Jaigīṣavya]] is described as one who was never angry nor joyful. In the Varāhapurāṇa (4. 14) it is stated that Kapila and [[Yogirat Jaiglṣarya|Yogirāṭ Jaigīṣavya]] came to king Aśvaśiras that had taken the avabhṛtha [[hath|bath]] after Aśvamedha and assumed the forms of Viṣṇu and Garuḍa respectively. It may be noted that on Y. S. II. 55 the [[hhāsya|bhāṣya]] quotes several views, but prefers that of [[Jaigigavya.|Jaigīṣavya.]] On Y. [[8.|S.]] III. 18,[^2286]] the bhāṣya mentions the dialogue between [[Avatya|Āvaṭya]] and [[Jaigisa vya,|Jaigīṣavya,]] where the latter's opinion is stated to be that from the point of view of [[hairulyn,|kaivalya,]] the happiness of contentment is also misery, though as compared to pleasures of sense contentment is supposed to be and may be called [[happiness|happiness.]]

In the Buddhacarita (XII) when Gautama (the future Buddha) approached a philosopher [[Arāda,|Arāḍa,]] the latter described to Gautama his idea of the path to [[moksa|mokṣa]] and mentioned [[Jaiglṣarya,|Jaigīṣavya,]] Janaka and [[Vrddha. Parāśara|Vṛddha-Parāśara]] as persons that had become liberated by following that [[path,2787|path.]][^2287]

From these references it follows that [[Jaigisavya|Jaigīṣavya]] was a great teacher of Yoga long before the Christian era and probably [[bad|had]] composed a work on Yoga not now available.

[[P1393]]Patañjali's sūtras (pp. 100–131); 'Aphorisms of Yoga' done into English by Shree Purohita Swami and Intro. by W. B. Yeats (Faber & Faber, London, 1937) with figures of [[siddhāsana,|Siddhāsana,]] [[Baddhapadmasana,|Baddhapadmāsana,]] [[Pascimottānāsada,|Paścimottānāsana,]] [[Bhujangāśana,|Bhujaṅgāsana,]] [[Viparit akaraṇl|Viparītakaraṇī]] and Matsyendrāsana; [['Bhāratiya|'Bhāratīya]] [[Mānaga-śāstra'|Mānasa-śāstra']] or [['Patañjala-yoga-darsana'|'Pātañjala-yoga-darśana']] (Sanskrit text, with a table of contents, errata, Introduction of 232 pages, and meaning and explanation pp. 787 in Marathi) in all 1051 pages, edited by Krishnaji [[Kashav|Keshav]] Kolhatkar and published by K. B. Dhavale, Bombay, 1951—a very exhaustive and useful work, though rather diffuse and marred by a few uncritical statements.

The number of works and papers on Yoga in general written by Indian and Western authors is very large. The present author has not read many of them. He may mention the following among those that he read or consulted; 'Rājayoga' (complete works of Vivekananda, ed. of 1946, Mayavati, [[vOL|vol.]] I. pp. 119–313); 'Yoga technique in the Great Epic' by W. Hopkins in JAOS vol. 22 for 1901 pp. 333–379; 'Yoga as a philosophy and [[religion|religion']] by Prof. [[8.|S.]] N. Das Gupta (London, 1924), and 'Yoga [[philosophy|philosophy']] (Uni. of Calcutta, 1930); Dr. J. W. [[Haūer's|Haüer's]] 'Die [[Anfrange|Anfänge]] der Yogapraxis im Alten [[Indien '( Stuttgart, 1922 )|Indien' (Stuttgart, 1922)]] and 'Der Yoga Als Heilweg nach den Indischen quellen Dargestellt', Stuttgart, 1932; this is a careful and systematic study of Yoga; this work (pp. 101–127) gives the transliterated text of the [[Yoga. Sūtra|Yoga-sūtra]] with translation in German not in serial order but by subjects (such as yogāṅgas, kriyāyoga, [[Isvaraprapidhāna);|Īśvarapraṇidhāna);]] Dr. Radhakrishnan's 'Indian Philosophy' vol. II. pp. 336–373 (London, 1931); 'The mysterious [[Kundalini'|Kuṇḍalinī']] by Dr. J. G. Rele ([[Taraporovala|Taraporevala]] and sons, Bombay, 1927); 'Yoga, the science of health' by Felix Guyot (in French), translated by J. Carling, London, 1937 (3rd ed.), expounding the practical principles of Haṭha-yoga; 'Yoga, a scientific[^2288] evaluation' by Dr. K. T. [[Bebanan,|Behanan,]] pub. by Macmillan & Co., New York, 1937 (the author studied at Kaivalya-dhāma for a year); 'Tibetan Yoga and secret [[dootrine'|doctrine']] by W. Y. Evans-Wentz (Oxford Uni. Press, 1935) and 'Tibetan Book of the Dead' by the same author (Oxford, 1927); 'A search in secret India' by Paul Brunton (London, 1947); [[Pąul|Paul]] Tuxen's 'the religions of [[India|India']] (Copenhagen, 1949);

[^2288]: This is one of the best modern books on Yoga. The present author is much impressed by Dr. [[Bebapan's|Behanan's]] sincerity, detailed and scientific treatment of Yoga and the very frank appraisal of some Yoga practices (pp. 225–249) at the end.

[[P1394]]'Tibetan Yoga' by Bernard Bromage (2nd ed. of 1939) pp. 108–111; [[.Yoga|'Yoga]] the method of re-integration' by Allain Danielou (London, 1949); the author [[atates|states]] that the exposition is based on yoga works and also on the teachings of living exponents; it is a useful book with six illustrations and 352 Sanskrit texts; [[* The|'The]] Tibetan Book of the Great Liberation' ed. by W. G. Evans-Wentz (Oxford Uni. Press, 1954); pp. XXIX–LXIV contain C. G. Jung's Psychological commentary and pp. 63–69 on astrology; 'Source-book of Indian Philosophy' by Dr. Radhakrishnan and C. A. Moore; gives translation of the whole yogasūtra with extracts from the [[bhāsya;|bhāṣya;]] [[Mercea|Mircea]] Eliade's 'Yoga, immortality and freedom' translated from French into English by Willard R. Trask (London, 1958); ‘Hathayoga, [[|']]an advanced method of physical education and concentration' by Prof. [[8. 8.|S. S.]] Goswami (L. N. Fowler, London, 1959); this is a very valuable book; it deals scientifically with yoga in all its aspects, but particularly with āsanas of which 108 photographs are given; [['Con centration'|'Concentration']] by Mouni Sadhu (London, 1959); 'The Lotus and the Robot' by A. Koestler (London, 1960).

Many editions of the [[Yogasutra|Yogasūtra]] of Patañjali with the bhāṣya of Vyāsa and the commentary (called [[Tattvavaisāradi)|Tattvavaiśāradī)]] of Vācaspati have been printed in India. I shall mention only two or three editions of the text of the Sūtra and the two commentaries viz. the edition by the late Pandit Rajaram Shastri Bodas, printed in beautiful type at the [[Nirnayasagara|Nirṇayasāgara]] Press in 1892 and the other, the [[Anandaśrama|Ānandāśrama]] edition of 1932 which contains the sūtra, bhāṣya, [[Vacaspati's|Vācaspati's]] commentary and also the commentary called Rājamārtaṇḍa of king Bhoja. In the Kashi Sanskrit series the Yogasūtra was printed (in 1930) with six commentaries viz. the Rājamārtaṇḍa of Bhojarāja, the [[Pradipikā|Pradīpikā]] of Bhāvā [[Ganesa,|Gaṇeśa,]] [[vitti|vṛtti]] by [[Nāgoji-bhatta,|Nāgoji-bhaṭṭa,]] the [[Maṛi. prabbā|Maṇiprabhā]] by Rāmānandayati, the Candrikā by Anantadeva and the Yoga-sudhākara by Sadāśivendra Sarasvatī.[^2288a]

The Yogasūtra, as compared with the sūtras of some other darśanas, is a brief one. It is divided into four [[pādus, vix.|pādas, viz.]]

[^2288a]: It should be noted that Bhojadeva omits Yogasūtra IV. 16 ([[me r a da AGHARTER aan Fan FENICE|न चैकचित्ततन्त्रं वस्तु तदप्रमाणकं तदा किं स्यात्]]) which is commented upon in the [[Vyasabhāṣya|Vyāsabhāṣya]] and by Vācaspati. On the other hand, Yogasūtra III. 20 ([[na ca tat-sālambadam tasyāvlṣayībbūtatvāt|na ca tat-sālambanaṁ tasyāviṣayībhūtatvāt]]) does not occur in the edition of Pandit Bodas Shastri and is not commented upon by Bhāvā [[Ganeta|Gaṇeśa]] and Nāgoji. The form of the sūtra [[fo|in]] Y. S. IV. 16 makes one feel some doubt about its [[genuineacas,|genuineness,]] but as it is explained by the bhāṣya and [[Vāçaspati,|Vācaspati,]] one has to accept it as [[genuing,|genuine.]]


[[P1395]][[Samadhi|Samādhi]] (concentration), [[Sadhana|Sādhana]] (means of attainment), Vibhūti (super-normal powers) and Kaivalya (aloofness, liberation). There are only 195 sūtras in all the pādas together (51+55+55+34).

Dr. Radhakrishnan in 'Indian Philosophy' ([[vol|vol.]] II. of the ed. of 1931 pp. 341–42) holds that the author of the Yogasūtra is not later than [[300 4. D.|300 A. D.]] Prof. [[8. N.|S. N.]] Das Gupta in 'History of Indian Philosophy' (vol. I. pp. 226–238) is in favour of the identity of the two Patañjalis and therefore holds that the author of the Yogasūtra flourished in the [[2nd century B.O.|2nd century B. C.]][^2289] The Sanskrit paper of Pandit R. [[Ramamurtiśarmā|Ramamūrtiśarmā]] in J. of [[Venkatesvara|Veṅkaṭeśvara]] Institute of Tirupati (vol. II. [[pp. 289-294|pp. 289–294]]) and Mr. Govind Das's brief note in I. A. vol. 44 (for 1915 p. 24) may also be read in this connection. Jacobi and (following him) Keith hold that the Yogasūtra (I. 40), meaning at the most 'the mastery of the Yogin extends from the [[minutost|minutest]] particle to the greatest magnitude imaginable',[^2290] refers to the atomic theory of the world. This is a good sample of the way in which even great Western writers [[road|read]] in simple words later theories and try to give late dates to early works. The Upaniṣads speak of the self as more minute [[then|than]] what is [[anu|aṇu]] and as greater than the great and the Mahābhārata also uses the same [[phraseology, There|phraseology. There]] is no convincing reason to suppose that the [[Yogasutra|Yogasūtra]] refers only to a theory of atoms propounded in the [[Vaibeṣika|Vaiśeṣika]] system and is not paraphrasing the words of the Upaniṣad and the Mahābhārata.

We have also to consider the early tradition embodied in an introductory verse in Bhojadeva's commentary (not later than [[1055 A, D.)|1055 A. D.)]] and mentioned by [[Cakrapāpi|Cakrapāṇi]] (commentator

[^2289]: Most scholars accept 2nd century B. C. as the date of the [[Mabābhāṣya,|Mahābhāṣya.]] Dr. D. C. Sircar in [[I. H.Q.|I. H. Q.]] vol. 15 (pp. 633–638) tries to show that the Mahābhāṣya cannot be placed [[earlier than the 2nd century A, D.|earlier than the 2nd century A. D.]] His [[argoments|arguments]] cannot be discussed here. The present author [[doas|does]] not agree with Dr. Sircar and holds that the arguments for placing the [[Mahābbāṣya|Mahābhāṣya]] in the 2nd century B. C. are far more weighty than those put forward by Dr. Sircar.

[^2290]: [[Guitirana ARCT \#Eramika maingat gerini isla. II, 30, श्वेता.उ. II. 20; 'अणोरणीयो महती महत्तरं तवात्मना पश्यति युक्त आत्मवान् । शान्तिपर्व 23233 (cr.ed.); the UTE 140 is a HTGUTHABETISFY aptiert: . seqratne शासनं विवधता पाससले कुर्वता पूर्ति राजमुगाङ्कसंज्ञकमपि ज्यातन्धवा धके। पाक्चेतोषपुषां मला फणिभृतां भञ्ष येनोवृतस्तस्य श्रीरणरड़मलस्पतेर्षाचो जयन्युज्ज्वला:n 5th Intro verse of Bhoja's výtti called state on Amax.|अणोरणीयान् महतो महीयानात्मास्य जन्तोर्निहितो गुहायाम् । कठोप. II. 20, श्वेता. उ. III. 20; 'अणोरणीयो महतो महत्तरं तदात्मना पश्यति युक्त आत्मवान् । शान्तिपर्व 232. 33 (cr. ed.); the योगसूत्र I. 40 is 'परमाणुपरममहत्त्वान्तोऽस्य वशीकारः '. शब्दानामनुशासनं विदधता पातञ्जले कुर्वता वृत्तिं राजमृगाङ्कसंज्ञकमपि व्यातन्वता वैद्यके। वाक्चेतोवपुषां मलः फणिभृतां भर्त्रेव येनोद्धृतस्तस्य श्रीरणरङ्गमल्लनृपतेर्वाचो जयन्त्युज्ज्वलाः॥ 5th Intro verse of Bhoja's vṛtti called राजमार्तण्ड on योगसूत्र.]]

[[P1396]]of the [[Carakasanhita 3291|Carakasaṁhitā]][^2291] (about 1060 A. D.) that [[Patakjall|Patañjali]] ([[doomed|deemed]] to be an [[avatūra|avatāra]] of [[Saṣa )|Śeṣa)]] composed works on Grammar, Yoga and Medicine.

The present author cannot deal at length in this volume with the two questions of identity and of the dates of the two works, as [[be|he]] is mainly concerned with the influence of Yoga on Dharmaśāstra works. He thinks that hardly any cogent [[argi. monts|arguments]] have been advanced to positively disprove the identity of the authors of the [[Mahabhāsya|Mahābhāṣya]] and of the [[Yogasutra.|Yogasūtra.]] Whether the revision of Caraka's work was done by the same Patañjali is very doubtful. In the [[Sāntiparva|Śāntiparva]] the founder of Cikitsita (medicine) is said to have been [[Kṭspātreya|Kṛṣṇātreya]] and not Caraka nor Patañjali. The [[Caraka-sambitā|Caraka-saṁhitā]] uses the expression [['iti ha smāba bhagavān.Atreya)'|'iti ha smāha bhagavān Ātreyaḥ']] in the beginning of its chapters. Caraka (in I. 1. 23 ff) states that the sage [[Bharad āja|Bharadvāja]] learnt Āyurveda from [[Indra His|Indra. His]] pupil was [[Punarvagu|Punarvasu]] Ātreya, who had six disciples viz. Agniveśa, [[Bheda,|Bheḍa,]] [[Jatākarpa,|Jatūkarṇa,]] [[Parasara,|Parāśara,]] [[Harita|Hārīta]] and [[Kṣarapāni.|Kṣārapāṇi.]] First, [[Agnivesa|Agniveśa]] composed a [[treatine|treatise]] on Āyurveda and read it to [[Xtrega|Ātreya]] and then [[Bheda|Bheḍa]] and others did so. In the chapter called [['Tisraisapiya'|'Tisraiṣaṇīya']] in [[Caraka-Bardhita|Caraka-saṁhitā]] (I. 11. 75) [[Krspātreya's|Kṛṣṇātreya's]] view is specially mentioned. Therefore, it appears that [[Kroṇātreya|Kṛṣṇātreya]][^2292] is different from Ātreya who is [[rovergntly|reverently]] mentioned at the beginning of Caraka's chapters. Even the Buddhacarita of Aśvaghoṣa mentions Ātreya as the first propounder of medical [[scienco.|science.]]

It is possible to argue that the tradition that Patañjali dealt with Yoga and Grammar is older than the [[Vakyapadiya|Vākyapadīya]] of [[Bhartphari.|Bhartṛhari.]] That work states in its first [[section 22%|section]][^2293] ([[Brahmakānda)|Brahmakāṇḍa)]] that the taints that affect the body, speech and

[^2291]: [[gaur-E181979 fra i fārgenront : Iotro. verse to com. on Another verse to the same effect is योगेन चिचस्य पदेन पाची मलं शरीरस्य चधकेन। योपाकरो प्रारं मुनीनां पतशाले aranfert cited io glad of formatura.|पातञ्जल-महाभाष्य-चरकप्रतिसंस्कृतैः । मनोवाक्कायदोषणां हर्त्रेऽहिपतये नमः ॥ Intro. verse to com. on चरक. Another verse to the same effect is योगेन चित्तस्य पदेन वाचां मलं शरीरस्य च वैद्यकेन । योऽपाकरोत् प्रवरं मुनीनां पतञ्जलिं प्राञ्जलिरानतोऽस्मि ॥ cited in योगवार्तिक of विज्ञानभिक्षु.]]

[^2292]: [[वेदविदेव भगवान वाहानि वृहस्पतिः। भार्गवो नीतिशाय च जमाव जमतो हितम् । गान्धर्ष नारदो घेवं भरद्वाजो धनुहम्। देवर्षिचरितं गायः कृष्णात्रेयश्चिकित्सितम्। ringarroganta domia naft: Im 203. 18-20 (cr. ed. - 210. 20-22 of Ch. ed.): referinta t fet: amani katire \# G 1. 50. Aśvaghosa is held to bave flourished in the first or 2nd century A. D.|वेदविद्विद्व भगवान् वेदाङ्गानि बृहस्पतिः । भार्गवो नीतिशास्त्रं च जगाद जगतो हितम् ॥ गान्धर्वं नारदो वेदं भरद्वाजो धनुर्ग्रहम् । देवर्षिचरितं गार्ग्यः कृष्णात्रेयश्चिकित्सितम् । न्यायतन्त्राण्यनेकानि तैस्तैरुक्तानि वादिभिः ॥ शान्ति 203. 18–20 (cr. ed. = 210. 20–22 of Ch. ed.) ; चिकित्सितं यच्च चकार नात्रिः पश्चात्तदात्रेय ऋषिर्जगाद ॥ बुद्धचरित I. 50. Aśvaghoṣa is held to have flourished in the first or 2nd century A. D.]]

[^2293]: [[rearrefraga1 AT: FATUAT: i praeerartigt विद्धयः॥ वाक्यपदीय I. 148; अलब्धमाघे गाम्भीर्यादुत्तान इच सौष्ठवात् । वाक्यपदीय II. 485 : तवे नाकाडे काथवाग्बुद्धिविषया ये मला त्यादि श्लोकेन भाग्यकामासा उका, ९ चैव माध्यमशंसति शासस्य शासकर्तुब टीकाकता महनोपवर्णिता दिलाराजाशीका.|कायवाग्बुद्धिविषया ये मलाः समवस्थिताः । चिकित्सा-लक्षणाध्यात्मशास्त्रैस्तेषां विशुद्धयः ॥ वाक्यपदीय I. 148; अलब्धगाधे गाम्भीर्यादुत्तान इव सौष्ठवात् । वाक्यपदीय II. 485; तदेवं ब्रह्मकाण्डे कायवाग्बुद्धिविषया ये मलाः - इत्यादि श्लोकेन भाष्यकारप्रशंसा उक्ता, इह चैव भाष्यप्रशंसैति शास्त्रस्य शास्त्रकर्तुश्च टीकाकृता महतोपवर्णिता । हेलाराज's टीका.]]

[[P1397]]intellect are purified ([[rospectively)|respectively)]] by the sciences of medicine, grammar and metaphysics. Then in the eulogy of the [[Maha bhāṣya|Mahābhāṣya]] it remarks [['alabdhagadhe|'alabhdagādhe]] [[gāmbhīryād-uttana|gāmbhīryād-uttāna]] iva [[sausthavāt',|sauṣṭhavāt',]] on which the commentator explains that in the verse from the [[Brahmakāṇda|Brahmakāṇḍa]] the author of the Mahābhāṣya is praised and in the other verse there is praise of the [[bhāsya|bhāṣya]] itself. From this it appears to follow that according to the commentator the [[Vākyapadiya|Vākyapadīya]] attributes the three śāstras on medicine, grammar and metaphysics ([[i, e.|i. e.]] Yoga) to Patañjali himself.

Even if it be held that the authors of the Yogasūtra and of the Mahābhāṣya are different persons, there are hardly any reasons for definitely assigning the author of the Yogasūtra to a date later than the 2nd or 3rd century A. D. The date of the [[Yogabhāsya|Yogabhāṣya]] of Vyāsa is of considerable importance in arriving at the probable date of the Yogasūtra. But the question of the date of the Yogabhāṣya is also debatable. The [[Vyasa|Vyāsa]] who composed the [[Yogabbāsya|Yogabhāṣya]] would have to be held as different from Vedavyāsa, the reputed author of the Mahābhārata.

The author of the Yogasūtra is, according to the tradition embodied in such comparatively early commentaries as that of [[Vācaspatimisra,|Vācaspatimiśra,]] said to be [[Patanjali.|Patañjali.]] Important questions arise as to his age and his identity with Patañjali, the author of the famous [[Mahabhāsya|Mahābhāṣya]] on [[Pāṇini'a|Pāṇini's]] grammar and the Vārtikas thereon. The grammarian Patañjali is generally held to have flourished about 150 [[B. O.|B. C.]] Therefore, the question of identity becomes important for the date of the [[Yogasutra.|Yogasūtra.]] Some scholars like Prof. B. Liebich, Dr. [[Hauer|Haüer]] (p. 98 of the work of 1932) and Prof. Das Gupta favour the identity of the two, but several others such as Jacobi, Keith, [[Wooda,|Woods,]] Renou are against it. Prof. [[Ronou|Renou]] (in [[I. E. Q. Vol.|I. H. Q. vol.]] XVI. pp. 586–591) examines the question from the grammatical point of [[vior,|view,]] points out that certain words like Pratyāhāra, Upasarga, Pratyaya are used in the Yogasūtra in senses entirely different from the senses of these words in the [[Mahabhāsya.|Mahābhāṣya.]] But as the subjects of the two works are entirely different, the same words may have acquired different senses. Similarly, Prof. Renou relies on breaches of grammatical rules (in the Yogasūtra I. 34), while he says that Patañjali in the [[Mahabhāṣya|Mahābhāṣya]] is very strict in following [[Pāpini's|Pāṇini's]] rules though [[Parini|Pāṇini]] himself is rarely not [[striot|strict]] in observing his own rules as in [['tat-prayojako hotusca!|'tat-prayojako hetuśca']] (I. 4. 55) which is [[Against|against]] his own sūtra [['trjakābhyām|'tṛjakābhyāṁ]] kartari' ([[IL.|II.]] 2. 15); but

[[P1398]][[Patanjali|Patañjali]] also employs such an expressions as 'aviravikanyāya'[^2294] (which should be [[avyavikanyayena|avyavikanyāyena]]) for which Patañjali [[ig oriticized|is criticized]] in the long [[Pūryapakṣa|Pūrvapakṣa]] on [[Vyakarana|Vyākaraṇa]] contained in the Tantravārtika and which is not explained away in the reply to the [[Puryapakṣa.|Pūrvapakṣa.]] It cannot be supposed that the [[Yogasutra|Yogasūtra]] for the first time settled the technical terms of [[Yoga Yoga|Yoga. Yoga]] terms had been evolving from [[Upanisad|Upaniṣad]] times and [[Patanjali|Patañjali]] only used them in the sense which they had acquired in the course of centuries. Prof. [[Ronou|Renou]] arrives at the conclusion that the Yogasūtra is later by several [[conturies|centuries]] than the [[Mahabhāsys,|Mahābhāṣya.]] Jacobi in his paper on 'the dates of the philosophical sūtras of the [[Brahmaṇas'|Brāhmaṇas']] (in JAOS, vol. 31 pp. 1–29) holds (p. 29) that [[be|the]] Yogasūtra must be later [[that|than]] the 5th century A. D. and, following Garbe, thinks that it is not improbable that the [[Vyasabhasya|Vyāsabhāṣya]] was composed in the 7th century [[AD.|A. D.]] The views of Jacobi are strongly criticized by Jwala Prasad in J. R. A. [[8.|S.]] for 1930 pp. 365–375. The present author disagrees with Jacobi and Prof. [[Ronou.|Renou.]]

The date of the [[Yogabbāṣya|Yogabhāṣya]] will have considerable bearing on the date of the Yogasūtra. The [[Yogabhāsya|Yogabhāṣya]] presupposes much literary activity on Yoga. It [[mentiona|mentions]] by name on Y. [[8.|S.]] II. 55 and on III. 18 [[Jaiglṣavya|Jaigīṣavya]] who is a prominent figure in the Mahābhārata as shown above (pp. 1367 and 1374). Vide also the story of Asita [[Dovala|Devala]] with whom [[Jaiglṣavya atayed|Jaigīṣavya stayed]] for many years as a [[bbiksu|bhikṣu]] and adept in Yoga ([[Salyaparva,|Śalyaparva,]] chap. 50). It has to be remembered that several interpretations of the same [[Yogasutra|Yogasūtra]] are noted in the bhāṣya ([[ag|as]] on II. 55). The Yogabhāṣya quotes several [[Kūrikūs|Kārikās]] and [[versos|verses]] dealing with the matters treated of in the Y. [[8.|S.]] as on Y. S. I. 28, 48, II. 5, 28 (on nine causes that lead to [[Vivekakbyati|Vivekākhyāti)]], II. 32, III. 6, [[JII, 15 (OD|III. 15 (on]] seven Cittadharmas of the [[apariltsta|apari-dṛṣṭa]] type). Besides these, several quotations in [[proso|prose]] are cited in the bhāṣya, many of which are attributed to [[Pancaśikha|Pañcaśikha]] by Vācaspati (vide pp. 1373–74 above), and some (as on II. 22, 30, 52) to āgamins (those who know the tradition or [[Voda).|Veda).]] Therefore, it follows that some centuries intervene between the Yogasūtra and its [[bhāgya.|bhāṣya.]]

The [[Bhagya|Bhāṣya]] on Y. [[8.|S.]] II. 42 quotes a verse with the words [[' tatha|'tathā]] coktam' (it has also been said), which is identical with

II. 4. 71) इति प्रत्ययोपविदोऽपि ङस् न कृतः ।' (p. 260, Ānan. ed.)]]

[[P1399]]a verse from the Śāntiparva.[^2295] It is most unlikely that an author will rely on his own work for supporting a proposition propounded in another work of his own. Besides, the Yogabhāṣya quotes on Y. S. I. 28 a verse, which is Viṣṇupurāṇa[^2296] VI. 6. 2. The Viṣṇupurāṇa is one of the earliest among extant Purāṇas (vide above pp. 907–909) and cannot be assigned to a date later than the 3rd century A. D. Therefore, the Yogabhāṣya which quotes the Mahābhārata and the Viṣṇupurāṇa may be assigned to about 4th century A. D. As argued above, the Y. S. would therefore have to be assigned to a date not later than the 2nd or 3rd century A. D. Though the present writer holds that the Yoga that is said to be refuted in V. S. II. 1. 3 is not that of the Yogasūtra but the Yoga found in the Śāntiparva, yet he is not prepared to place the present Yogasūtra earlier than the 2nd century B. C. Mr. K. K. Kolhatkar in his very exhaustive and learned Introduction (in Marathi) asserts (on p. 126) that the Yogasūtra is later by 3000 years than the Brahmasūtra and that the Bhagavadgītā was composed about 5000 years before the present day (p. 224). But he adduces no evidence for such an ancient date for that work. He probably relies on the traditions that Vyāsa, the reputed author of the Mahābhārata (including the Gītā), flourished towards the end of the Dvāpara age and that Kaliyuga started in 3179 B. C. Modern critical scholarship does not accept these traditional datings.

Not only do some of the Upaniṣads contain references to Yoga technique and practices, but the Mahābhārata also dilates upon matters that pertain to the domain of Yoga. A few examples may be referred to here (the cr. ed. of the Mahābhārata is relied upon). In the Śāntiparva, chap. 232 (241 of Ch. ed.)

[[P1400]]it is [[stated 239|stated]][^2297] that kāma (desires), krodha (anger), lobha (covetousness), bhaya (fear) and svapna (sleep) are the five [[dosas ( disturbances ),|doṣas (disturbances),]] in the path of Yoga and then it [[proscribos|prescribes]] means for mastering them. An important and striking assertion in that chapter is that even a person of the lowest [[rurṇa ( class )|varṇa (class)]] or [[&|a]] woman who is intent on dharma may reach the highest goal by following this path[^2298] (of Yoga). The same chapter [[laya|lays]] down (in verse 25) the proper places for the residence of a yogin and for the practice of Yoga, viz. untenanted mountains and caves, temples, vacant houses, in order that ekāgratā ([[one pointedness|one-pointedness]] of mind) may be achieved and that the yogin should treat in the same way a person who commends him or who speaks ill of him and should not contemplate the befalling of good or evil on any one. Chapter 289 of the [[Santiparva|Śāntiparva]] refers to [[Dhārapā ( verge 37 )|Dhāraṇā (verse 37)]] and states that a yogin that [[hag|has]] attained[^2299] spiritual power may transfer himself to thousands of other bodies and move about in those bodies on the earth and that this path (of Yoga) is regarded as difficult of access even to wise brāhmaṇas and no one can tread it with ease and that [[ona|one]] can stand easily on the sharp edges of razors, but to abide in [[Dhāraña|Dhāraṇā]] of Yoga is difficult for those whose souls are not purified. Śāntiparva 304. 1 states that there is no knowledge equal to [[Saṅkhya|Sāṅkhya]] and no spiritual power equal to Yoga; it further says that Yoga is eightfold ([[vorge 7 )|verse 7)]] and in verse 9 mentions

[[P1401]][[Dharana|Dhāraṇā]] and Prāṇāyāma. The [[Asvamedhikaparva|Āśvamedhikaparva]] 19. 17 (Ch. ed.) appears to refer to pratyāhāra.[^2300]

There is also striking similarity between the [[Bhagavadgitā|Bhagavadgītā]] and the Yogasūtra.[^2301] For example, the definition of Yoga in the Yogasūtra as the 'mastery over (or elimination of) the functions (or fluctuations) of the mind' has a close parallel in the [[Gitā.|Gītā.]] The [[Gitā|Gītā]] insists on the yogin being [['aparigraba'|'aparigraha']] (VI. 10); [['aparigpha'|'aparigraha']] is one of the five yamas (Y. S. [[II, 30 ).|II. 30).]] Similarly, the seat and posture (āsana) in which a yogin is to practise must be stable (this refers to the seat) and comfortable (says [[Yogasūtra );|Yogasūtra);]] the [[Gitā|Gītā]] says the same thing in greater detail. In [[VIII, 12|VIII. 12]] the [[Gitā|Gītā]] speaks of [[Yogadharapā.|Yogadhāraṇā.]] The [[Gitā|Gītā]] says (in [[VI, 35 )|VI. 35)]] that the mind is certainly restless and difficult to control, but it can be controlled by practice and passionlessness and the Y. S. [[(I. 12 )|(I. 12)]] speaks of the same two means. The [[Gita|Gītā]] (in V. [[4-5)|4–5)]] emphasizes that it is the ignorant that regard [[Saṅkhya|Sāṅkhya]] and Yoga as different, that he who applies himself or stands committed to one of these secures the fruit held out by both and that he who [[bees|sees]] both ways as identical [[soos|sees]] truly. [[Hore|Here]] [[Saṅkhya|Sāṅkhya]] means (sannyāsa) 'renunciation' and Yoga means [[' Karma yoga',|'Karma-yoga'.]]

The Yogasūtra of Patañjali nowhere expressly sets out its scheme of the evolution of the world. But it contains enough material to enable us to hold that it presupposes and accepts some of the [[Saṅkhya|Sāṅkhya]] doctrines such as the theory of [[Pradhana,|Pradhāna,]] the three guṇas and their characteristics, the real nature of the individual self and kaivalya (the state of the soul in final liberation). This may be established by a few references to [[Y.S.|Y. S.]] [[Y, S.|Y. S.]] III. 48 describes the results that ensue to the yogin from subjugation or mastery of the organs (indriyas), one of which is

[[P1402]]'pradhānajaya'[^2302] (subjugation of [[Pradhana. the|Pradhāna, the]] primary cause of the world according to the [[Bankhya).|Sāṅkhya).]] The [[Y. 8.|Y. S.]] nowhere describes what [[Pradhana|Pradhāna]] is and what its evolutes are. Therefore, it follows that it takes over from the Sāṅkhya all that is [[geid|said]] about [[Pradhana.|Pradhāna.]] About the individual soul the [[Y. 8.|Y. S.]] states 'the seer [[(Purusa)|(Puruṣa)]] [[exiats zz03|exists]][^2303] as merely pure capacity of awareness (or as the mere power of seeing) and although pure ([[changeles|changeless]] or undefiled or free from any taints) appears as if he [[soos|sees]] all [[tiporiences that really pertain to Buddhi).'|experiences (that really pertain to Buddhi)'.]] The characteristics of the three guṇas (sattva, rajas and tamas) are clearly and [[succintly 1305|succinctly]][^2304] stated in [[Y. 8.|Y. S.]] as in [[8.|S.]] Kārikā 13, when it [[gaye *tbe|says 'the]] seen (phenomenal universe) is characterized by [[light (sattva ),|light (sattva),]] [[activity (rajas )|activity (rajas)]] and inertia or dullness (tamas), it is the essence of the elements and organs and it exists for the purpose of giving experience and liberation (to the soul)'. The guṇas are frequently referred to in [[Y. 8. 1. 16,|Y. S. I. 16,]] IV. 13, 32, 34 and [[satraguna|sattvaguṇa]] in Y. S. [[II, 41,|II. 41,]] III. 35, 49 and 55. The [[Y. 8.|Y. S.]] postulates three [[pramūnas (in 1.7),|pramāṇas (in I. 7),]] but does not define them; the [[8.|S.]] [[Kāriki (4-6)|Kārikā (4–6)]] mentions the same three [[pramāgas|pramāṇas]] and briefly defines them. They both agree about the plurality of souls. It may further be noted that [[Vyasabhāsya|Vyāsabhāṣya]] on Y. S. is full of Sāṅkhya doctrines and quotes, according to Vācaspati, [[Pancasikha|Pañcaśikha]] twelve times and [[Ṣasṭitantra|Ṣaṣṭitantra]] once (vide above p. 1374 and [[. 2250 ).|n. 2250).]]

In spite of the fact that the Yogasūtra accepts some of the fundamental doctrines of the [[Bankhya,|Sāṅkhya,]] there are certain points of difference between the two. The standard Sāṅkhya finds [[zo placa|no place]] for God ([[Isvara )|Īśvara)]] i. e. it is frankly atheistic ([[ if| (if]] theism means belief in God who is the creator of the universe and regulator of rewards and punishments), while Yoga finds a place for God in [[Y. 8. 1. 23-29,|Y. S. I. 23–29,]] though not a basic one, but only secondary, probably as a concession to popular feelings and belief. The

[[P1403]][[Y. 8,|Y. S.]] nowhere expressly says that [[Isvara|Īśvara]] is the creator of the world and all that it says about Him is that in him omniscience is at its highest, that He was the teacher of primal sages and that by repetition of the [[mystio|mystic]] syllable 'om' and reflection on it the yogin attains correct knowledge of the real nature of the self. In the next place, though the summum bonum in both systems is called kaivalya ([[8.|S.]] Kārikā 64, 68 and [[Y. 8. III, 50, 55, IV, 34 ),|Y. S. III. 50, 55, IV. 34),]] yet the Sāṅkhya does not [[get|set]] out any elaborate discipline for attaining it except correct knowledge, that is, it is metaphysical and rational, while the [[Y, S.|Y. S.]] gives an elaborate treatment on mind discipline, insists on effort rather than [[inere|mere]] jñāna, attaches great importance to prāṇāyāma and meditation (dhyāna) and is psychological.

The Sāṅkhya postulated that an intellectual understanding of the nature of [[purusa|puruṣa]] and of [[praksti|prakṛti]] (or [[gunas )|guṇas)]] and of the difference between the two was sufficient for the emancipation of the individual self from the liability to rebirth, while yoga, on the other hand, was not content with this philosophic easy-going mental frame and emphasized systematic training of the will and emotions. In both Sāṅkhya and Yoga each individual soul is eternal and his destiny is to become free from the influence of Prakṛti and its evolutes and to remain for ever the same ([[vig.|viz.]] pure intelligence). Herein they differ from the Advaita Vedānta, [[According|according]] to which the final destiny of the self is to become absorbed in and one with brahma.

There is another matter for consideration. In the [[Yājña valkya-smrti, Yajnavalkya|Yājñavalkya-smṛti, Yājñavalkya]] is put forward as saying that the [[Boul|soul]] shining like a lamp in the heart should be realized, that on such realization the soul is not born again and as adding that for the purpose of attaining to Yoga one should understand the [[Aranyaka|Āraṇyaka]][^2305] which 'I received from the Sun and also the

III. 110; याज्ञवल्क्यो महायोगी दृष्ट्वाथ तपसा हरम् । चकार तन्नियोगेन योगशास्त्रमनुत्तमम् ॥ कूर्म I. 25. 44 (= B. [[P1404]]Yogaśāstra propounded by me.' In the [[Kūrmapurana it 18 Baid.|Kūrmapurāṇa it is said]] that [[Yajñavalkya|Yājñavalkya]] composed Yogaśāstra at the order of [[Hara'and|Hara and]] the [[Vispupurāpa (IV. 4 107)|Viṣṇupurāṇa (IV. 4. 107)]] states that [[Hirapyanābha|Hiraṇyanābha]] attained the knowledge of Yoga from the great [[Yoglāvara Yajnavalkya|Yogīśvara Yājñavalkya]] who was a pupil of Jaimini. In the Bṛhadāraṇyaka Up. (II. 4) [[Yajñavalkya|Yājñavalkya]] tells his wife [[Maitreyi|Maitreyī]] (who hankered after immortality and cared [[daught|naught]] for worldly goods) that he would expound to her the path to immortality and the very first sentence calls upon her to cultivate [['nididhyāga' (i. e, dhyana)|'nididhyāsa' (i. e. dhyāna)]] and the first part of his discourse [[onds|ends]] with the memorable words [[(ātmā vā are drastavyaḥ śrotavyo mantavyo nididhy. sitavyah, Bṛ, Up. II, 4.5).|('ātmā vā are draṣṭavyaḥ śrotavyo mantavyo nididhyāsitavyaḥ', Bṛ. Up. II. 4. 5).]] What work is meant by the [[Yoga Śāstra|Yogaśāstra]] composed by Yāj. is a debatable point. There are three works (other than the [[Yāj. smrti)|Yāj. smṛti)]] connected with the name of Yājñavalkya viz. ... [[Vļddha-Yājñavalkya, Yoga-Yajñavalkya and Bṭbad-yogi-yājñavalkya|Vṛddha-Yājñavalkya, Yoga-Yājñavalkya and Bṛhad-yogi-yājñavalkya]] (vide H. of Dh. [[Vol I. p. 188-190).|vol. I. pp. 188–190).]] The last has [[beon|been]] published by [[Kaivalyadbama,|Kaivalyadhama,]] Lonavla (1951), the [[Yoga-Yaj.|Yoga-Yāj.]] was published in T. S. S. (1938) based on a single ms., then in the JBBRAS ([[vol. 28 and 29 )|vol. 28 and 29)]] by Shri P. C. Diwanji and now available in book form (1954). This last is in the form of a dialogue between the great yogin [[Yājöavalkya|Yājñavalkya]] and [[Gārgi (who is identified with Maitreyi|Gārgī (who is identified with Maitreyī]] by Shri Diwanji on [[pp. 27-28 of his ed. of 1954 )|pp. 27–28 of his ed. of 1954)]] and sages and learned brāhmaṇas. Yājñavalkya narrates what he learnt from Brahmā. On Yāj. III. 110 the [[Dipakalikā of Sulapāṇi|Dīpakalikā of Śūlapāṇi]] remarks that the [[Yogasāstra referred to is called. Yogiyājñavalkya'.|Yogaśāstra referred to is called 'Yogiyājñavalkya'.]] But this again leads to uncertainty as both the works viz. that published by [[Kaivalya dhama|Kaivalyadhama]] and the one by Mr. Diwanji are [[80|so]] referred to as Yogi (or Yoga) [[Yajñavalkya|Yājñavalkya]] in several digests. Mr. [[Diwanjia306|Diwanji]][^2306] tries hard to establish that the work published by [[hin|him]] 'has a better claim to be adjudged the specific work on Yoga referred to in [[Yaj. III. 113.'|Yāj. III. 113.']] I think that the claim is not at all justified. The work edited by Mr. Diwanji no doubt contains a good deal of Yoga knowledge; while the work published by Kaivalyadhama also contains [[&|a]] good deal of Yoga material, though not as much as the work edited by Mr. Diwanji [[containg.|contains.]] It would be irrelevant to discuss here all the points made by Mr. Diwanji. The [[procent|present]] author is convinced that the work sponsored by Mr. Diwanji is not the work referred to by [[Yājóavalkya (in his smrti|Yājñavalkya (in his smṛti]] III. 110). It is a compilation of a later period. Some striking

[^2306]: Vide [[Sbri|Shri]] P. C. Diwanji's lengthy paper on [['Brbad-Yogi Yajia vaikya|'Bṛhad-Yogi Yājñavalkya]] and [[Yoga-Yājöavalkya' 10 ABQRI|Yoga-Yājñavalkya' in ABORI]] vol. 34 [[pp. 1-29.|pp. 1–29.]]

[[P1405]]matters alone may be pointed out to show that the [[Yoga Yājñavalkya oould|Yoga-Yājñavalkya could]] not have been the work of the author of the [[Bpbadāraṇyaka|Bṛhadāraṇyaka]] and the Yogaśāstra (as stated in Yāj. III. [[110 )|110)]] and of [[Yaj, smrti.|Yāj. smṛti.]] In the Bṛ. Up. II. 4. 1 and [[IV.5.1|IV. 5. 1]] ([[Yājña valkyasya|Yājñavalkyasya]] dve bhārye [[babhūvatur-Maitreyi ca Katyayani ca )|babhūvatur-Maitreyī ca Kātyāyanī ca)]] it is expressly stated that [[Yajāavalkya|Yājñavalkya]] had two wives, [[(philosophy minded) Maitreyi and Kātýāyan!|(philosophy-minded) Maitreyī and Kātyāyanī]] (worldly-minded). [[Maitreyi|Maitreyī]] desired such knowledge as would lead to immortality and she asks questions, in all of which she addresses [[Yājsavalkya|Yājñavalkya]] as [[' bhagavān'|'bhagavān']] (in Bṛ. Up. II. 4. 3 and 13 and IV. 5. 4, 14) and never as mere Yājñavalkya. On the other hand, [[Gārgi|Gārgī]] in the [[Bṛ, Up,|Bṛ. Up.]] is called [[Vacaknavi (in III. 6. 1, 1II. 8.1|Vācaknavī (in III. 6. 1, III. 8. 1]] and [[12 ),|12),]] is not [[Yajnavalkya's|Yājñavalkya's]] wife but a pert and rationalistic woman inquirer like [[Afvala, Artabhāga,|Aśvala, Ārtabhāga,]] Bhujyu Lāhyāyani, Uṣasta Cākrāyaṇa, Kahola (all present in the court of Janaka), who including [[Gārgi|Gārgī]] questioned the claim of [[Yājöavalkya|Yājñavalkya]] to be regarded as [[' brahmiṣtha'.|'brahmiṣṭha'.]] In Bṛ. Up. [[III, 6.1|III. 6. 1]] when [[Gārgi|Gārgī]] pursues her ratiocination too far (in matters depending upon āgama and being beyond [[reasoning )|reasoning)]] Yājñavalkya [[reprimande|reprimands]] her and says that she would die ([[lit:|lit.]] her head would fall away) if she pursued the subject in [[& pure logistic|a pure logistic]] way. All [[those|these]] inquirers address Yājñavalkya as mere Yājñavalkya without any [[honorifio|honorific]] epithet like 'bhagavān' and [[Gārgi|Gārgī]] does the same (in Bṛ. Up. [[III. 6, 1,|III. 6. 1,]] III. 8. 2–6). [[Aoc, to Yāj. Smrti|Acc. to Yāj. Smṛti]] III. 110 the [[Br. Up., a Yoga. śāstra and the smrti|Bṛ. Up., a Yogaśāstra and the smṛti]] are the works of one man (whose two wives were [[Maitreyl and Katyayani)|Maitreyī and Kātyāyanī)]] and who had a philosophical skirmish with [[Gārgl Vācaknavi ( according to the Br. Up.).|Gārgī Vācaknavī (according to the Bṛ. Up.).]] The present Yogayājñavalkya (ed. by Mr. Diwanji) makes [[Gārgi|Gārgī]] wife of Yājñavalkya[^2307] ([[vadhūḥ).|(vadhūḥ).]] One would like to know whether [[Yaj.|Yāj.]] had three wives, as [[Bș.|Bṛ.]] Up. says he had only two. Mr. Diwanji ([[pp. 27-28|pp. 27–28]] of the [[ed,|ed.]] of 1954) tries to make light of this [[discrepanoy|discrepancy]] and regards [[Maitreyi|Maitreyī]] as another name of [[Gārgi.|Gārgī.]] We are here [[con cerned|concerned]] not with pure Yoga [[dootrines|doctrines]] but with the question

[^2307]: The [[l . I. 6-7 are ASTUTENTE T EMAT : Harry महाभागा गार्गी च ब्रह्मविद्वरा सभामध्यगता चेयमृषीणामुग्रतेजसाम्। प्रणम्य दण्डवमो ray Aura N.|योगया. I. 6–7 are समेत्यगुणसंपन्नं नारीणामुत्तमा वधूः । मैत्रेयी च महाभागा गार्गी च ब्रह्मविद्वरा ॥ सभामध्यगता चेयमृषीणामुग्रतेजसाम् । प्रणम्य दण्डवद् भूमौ गार्ग्येतद् वाक्यमब्रवीत् ॥]] The two [[as|चs]] may be noted; that would ordinarily show that [[it and it|मैत्रेयी and गार्गी]] were different. It might be argued that having already learnt from [[Yāj. (io Bṛ. Up.)|Yāj. (in Bṛ. Up.)]] Maitreyī was present but took [[do|no]] part in the discussion and Gārgī alone asked questions. Verse 6 of chap. I [[could. be|could be]] considered as using the words [[उत्तमा व tor मैत्रेयी|उत्तमा वधूः for मैत्रेयी]] and the words [[महाभांगी and are as applicable to troll,|महाभागा and ब्रह्मविद्वरा as applicable to गार्गी,]] but this is made impossible by the text which [[lo|in]] I. 43 and IV. 5 refers to [[Girgi|Gārgī]] as the wife (bhāryā) of [[Yāj. and phe|Yāj. and she]] is addressed as 'priye' (IV. 7), [['vapārohe' pic.|'varārohe' etc.]]

[[P1406]]whether a work attributed to [[Yaj.|Yāj.]] which makes [[Gārgi|Gārgī]] the wife of the ancient [[Yājāavalkya (|Yājñavalkya (]]when the Upaniṣad makes her only a more or [[loss|less]] insolent woman) can be regarded as the work of the same Yāj. who propounded brahmavidyā in the [[Br. Up.|Bṛ. Up.]] and is supposed to have composed the Yāj. [[smrti.|smṛti.]] This [[ono circums bance|one circumstance]] alone is [[onough|enough]] to brand the [[Yoga-Yajñavalkya (|Yoga-Yājñavalkya (]]of Mr. Diwanji) as a fabrication sought to be passed off as an ancient work. If identity was really meant the [[verso|verse]] could easily [[bave|have]] been read as 'Maitreyyākhyā mahābhāgā' without any metrical fault. Therefore, it is not possible to hold that Yogayājñavalkya is the Yogaśāstra composed by Yājñavalkya before the [[Smrti|Smṛti]] going under his name was composed. A few other decisive grounds may also be urged. The work edited by Mr. Diwanji [[moptions|mentions]] Tantras [[( in V. 10)|(in V. 10)]] and [[Tantrikas (in VIII. 4 and 25 ).|Tāntrikas (in VIII. 4 and 25).]] The Yāj. [[smrti|smṛti]] nowhere mentions these two and is free from the peculiar [[Tantrik|Tāntrik]] words or technique. Therefore, the present Yogayājñavalkya edited by Mr. Diwanji was composed long after the Yāj. [[smrti|smṛti]] and probably in the 8th century or after when [[Tantrik|Tāntrik]] rites and works had become common. One more important matter may be adverted to. The Yāj. [[sınti|smṛti]] and the Yoga-Yājñavalkya [[( ed. by Mr. Diwanji )|(ed. by Mr. Diwanji)]] both enumerate ten yamas and ten niyamas. But the two diverge materially in the ten names as the note below will [[show, 2008|show.]][^2308] The number of yamas and niyamas differs in different works, but if the Yāj. [[smrti|smṛti]] and Yoga-Yājñavalkya were the works of the same author, the differences in the ten names would not have [[ocourred|occurred]] at all. Therefore, the author of the Yāj. [[empti|smṛti]] is quite different from the author of the Y. Y. There is no evidence for holding that the latter flourished before the [[Sth|8th]] or 9th century A. D.

A good deal has been said by Mr. Diwanji in his paper on [[' * Bṛbad-yogi-yājñavalkya|'Bṛhad-yogi-yājñavalkya]] and [[Yoga-Yajñavalkya'|Yoga-Yājñavalkya']] in [[ABORI, VOL.|ABORI, vol.]] XXXIV (1953) [[pp. 1-29,|pp. 1–29,]] in his preface to [[Yoga-Yājāavalky&|Yoga-Yājñavalkya]] in [[JBBRAS, volumes XXXVILI|JBBRAS, volumes XXVIII]] and XXXIX [[pp. 103-106|pp. 103–106]] and by Swami Kuvalayanand in his reply in ABORI, vol. XXXVII for

[[P1407]]1957 [[pp. 279-289|pp. 279–289]] and in the paper [['the real Yogayajāavalkya: smrti'|'the real Yogayājñavalkya-smṛti']] in the journal [['Yogamimārnsa' VOL VII,|'Yogamīmāṁsā' vol. VII,]] No. 2 (and also published separately as a pamphlet in July 1958). It would not be relevant in this work to discuss the points in dispute between Mr. Diwanji and Swami Kuvalayanand. In the [[bhāsya|bhāṣya]] on the [[Svetāśvataropaniṣad|Śvetāśvataropaniṣad]] attributed to [[Saṅkarācārya|Śaṅkarācārya]][^2309] (published by [[Anan, Press)|Ānan. Press)]] on p. 28 four [[Ferses|verses]] and a half are quoted from [[Yogi-Yajśavalkya,|Yogi-Yājñavalkya,]] none of which is found either in Bṛ. Y. Y. or Y. Y. Besides on [[Sv. Up. II. 9|Śv. Up. II. 9]] ([[pp. 42-44)|pp. 42–44)]] 29 verses on Yoga matters are quoted in the [[bhāsya,|bhāṣya,]] but the name of the author or work whence the quotations are taken is not mentioned. Not one of the 29 verses wholly occurs in Mr. [[Diwanji's Y, Y.|Diwanji's Y. Y.]] He is able to point out only five or six half verses as occurring in [[Y, Y.|Y. Y.]] out of the 29 [[versos,|verses,]] while a whole [[yerse|verse]] quoted in the [[bhāsya|bhāṣya]] [[(viz.'prāṇāyāmair' &c )|(viz. 'prāṇāyāmair' &c)]] occurs in Bṛ. Y. Y. (8. [[32 ). (It|32). It]] may be further pointed out that Aparārka and the [[Smrticandrika|Smṛticandrikā]] quote in all about 100 verses from Yogi (or Yoga-) [[Yājśavalky&|Yājñavalkya]] which are found in [[Bs. Y. Y.,|Bṛ. Y. Y.,]] but not in Y. Y. The Kṛtyakalpataru (on [[Mokṣakāṇda alone )|Mokṣakāṇḍa alone)]] quotes (on [[pp. 146, 149, 166, 171, 196-197)|pp. 146, 149, 166, 171, 196–197)]] about 70 verses from [[Yogi-yājāvalky&|Yogi-yājñavalkya]] which are found in chapters 2, 8, 9 and 11 of the [[Br. Y. Y,|Bṛ. Y. Y.]] Mr. Diwanji has not been able to show that verses of the Y. Y. [[(ed, by him )|(ed. by him)]] are cited anywhere in the above three digests. Mr. Bhabatosh [[Bhattacarya|Bhattacharya]] in his paper on [[Yogi-Yajāavalkya-gmṛti|Yogi-Yājñavalkya-smṛti]] and its utilization in the medieval digests of Bengal and Mithila (in [[J.G. J. R. I, vol XV for 1958 pp. 135–140 )|J. G. J. R. I. vol. XV for 1958 pp. 135–140)]] points out that king Ballālasena of Bengal ([[1158–1179 A, D.)|1158–1179 A. D.)]] in his Dānasāgara contains a fairly large number of quotations from the [[Bșhad-Yogi Yaj. (pub.|Bṛhad-Yogi Yāj. (pub.]] by Kaivalya-dhama). It appears that [[Viśvarūpa2210|Viśvarūpa]][^2310] (first half of 9th century A.D.) quotes a half verse from the [[Br. Y.Y.|Bṛ. Y. Y.]] and remarks that the work is composed by the author of the Yāj.

[^2309]: The authenticity of the [[bbāsya|bhāṣya]] as the great [[Saṅkarācārya's|Śaṅkarācārya's]] work is extremely doubtful. [[Io|In]] the whole of the extensive [[bhāsya|bhāṣya]] on the [[Brahma sūtra Saṅkarācārya quotes ao Parāṇa|Brahmasūtra Śaṅkarācārya quotes no Purāṇa]] by name but cites only a very few verses with the words [['iti parāṇe', But|'iti purāṇe'. But]] in the [[bhāsya|bhāṣya]] on the [[Sv. Up, of . only|Śv. Up. of only]] 76 printed pages over 30 verses from [[Brahmapurana,|Brahmapurāṇa,]] about 30 [[verdes|verses]] from [[Vispupurāṇa,|Viṣṇupurāṇa,]] about a dozen from the [[Lingapurāṇa|Liṅgapurāṇa]] and about half a dozen [[versos|verses]] from the Śivadharmottara (on pp. 7, 34, 35) are cited.

VII. 6 steget fogara a ore Thanterie mecref p. 235 ( RT) ... I frenta farat C) and more I. p. 129 rends as Stech doeg.|On स्नानमब्दैवतैर्मन्त्रैर्मार्जनं प्राणसंयमः । या. स्मृति I. 22, विश्वरूप comments 'स्नानं प्रत्यहं कुर्यात् । तस्य च विधिराचार्येणैवान्यत्राभिहितः, विद्यमाने प्रभूते हि नाल्पे स्नाने समाचरेत्' इति. [[P1408]][[smati himself Therefore,|smṛti himself. Therefore,]] the [[Br. Y.Y.|Bṛ. Y. Y.]] is an early work and cannot be assigned to a [[dato|date]] later than the 7th century A.D.; while Y.Y. is a much later [[oompilation|compilation]] composed in the 8th or 9th century or even later. The present author is not, however, prepared to hold that [[Br. Y. Y.|Bṛ. Y. Y.]] is the Yogaśāstra of Yāj. mentioned in Yāj. [[sifti III, 110,|smṛti III. 110,]] since the Yoga material contained in it is meagre as compared with the [[Smrti|Smṛti]] material. The present author [[ia|is]] glad to find that on [[pp. 7-8|pp. 7–8]] of the Sanskrit Preface to the edition of the [[Bșhad-Yogi-Yājñavalkya-smrti|Bṛhad-Yogi-Yājñavalkya-smṛti]] the editors express grave doubts about the identity of the author of the Yāj. smṛti with the author of the work edited by them.

The [[Yogavāsiṣtha|Yogavāsiṣṭha]] is an extensive work in 32000 [[ślokag (ope sloka|Ślokas (one śloka]] meaning 32 syllables) printed in two volumes with [[& gomventary by Anandabodha by the Nirn.|a commentary by Ānandabodha by the Nirṇ.]] Press. It is really an eclectic work [[embodying 21|embodying]][^2311] the tenets of the [[Gitā|Gītā]] on anāsakti, the tenets of the Trika system of Kashmir, of Advaita [[Vedanta|Vedānta]] &c. Its text appears to have been added to from time to time. Controversies have raged about its date and its contribution to Indian philosophy. The present writer holds on reading the contributions[^2312] that the extant [[Yogavāsiṣtha|Yogavāsiṣṭha]] is a late work and was composed some time between the 11th and 13th century A. D.

It is now necessary to furnish a brief outline of the main teachings of Y. S. Though the work is comparatively small, [[it!|its]] rendering in English requires to be amplified in order that it may be understandable. Only the most important and [[instruc tive|instructive]] matters are set out here.

[[Yogi|Yoga]] is defined (2nd sūtra) as the elimination of or mastery over the functions (activities) or fluctuations of the mind. This is provisionally explained by [[Vyasa ( on 1. 1.)|Vyāsa (on I. 1.)]] as

[^2311]: Though there is [[aot macb|not much]] compact teaching on [[paro|pure]] Yoga, the योगवासिष्ठ [[bere|here]] and there [[bas|has]] remarks on योग. For example, the उपशमप्रकरण [[cbap.|chap.]] 78 verse 8 states: [[e t POURTET A wr y Traforati pa शान सम्यगवेक्षणम् ..|द्वौ क्रमौ चित्तनाशस्य योगो ज्ञानं च राघव । योगस्तद्वृत्तिरोधो हि ज्ञानं सम्यगवेक्षणम् ॥]]

[^2312]: Vide Dr. Atreya's thesis on the [['Philosopby of Yogavāsigthao publisbed in 1936 at Adyar by the Theosophic Publisbing House ( he holds that it belongs to 6tb|'Philosophy of Yogavāsiṣṭha' published in 1936 at Adyar by the Theosophic Publishing House (he holds that it belongs to 6th]] century A. D.); [[I, H. &.|I. H. Q.]] vol. 24 [[pp. 201-212 (Prof. S. P. Bbattacharya ),|pp. 201–212 (Prof. S. P. Bhattacharya),]] I. H. Q. vol. 25 [[pp. 132-134 (Dr. D. C. Sarkar);|pp. 132–134 (Dr. D. C. Sircar);]] Mr. Diwanji's papers in Pro. of 7th All India O. Conf. [[pp. 15-30, P. O. III. 1 pp. 29-44,|pp. 15–30, P. O. III. 1 pp. 29–44,]] [[N, 1. A. vol. I pp. 697-715 (on Lagba-yogavāsipfha ), JOR, Mad.|N. I. A. vol. I pp. 697–715 (on Laghu-yogavāsiṣṭha), JOR, Mad.]] vol. XIII. [[pp. 71-82 and 118-128 (Dr, Raghavan ),|pp. 71–82 and 118–128 (Dr. Raghavan),]] ABORI vol. 32 [[pp. 130-145,Stages of the mind"|pp. 130–145.]]

[[P1409]][[Samadhi.|Samādhi.]] The different stages [[viz. bhūmis) of the mind ase|(viz. bhūmis) of the mind are]] five, being restless [[(ksipta), infatuated (mugdha or mudha), distracted (viksipta), one-pointed (ekagra) and controlled (niruddha.).|(kṣipta), infatuated (mugdha or mūḍha), distracted (vikṣipta), one-pointed (ekāgra) and controlled (niruddha).]]

In this connection [[(i.e. on the topic of bhumis) the present . author|(i. e. on the topic of bhūmis) the present author]] has to bring to the notice of readers a paper by Shri Kuvalayānanda contributed to the [[Yogamlmāmgā vol VI.|Yogamīmāṁsā vol. VI.]] No. 4 on 'Traditional reading of Pātañjala-yogasūtra III. 11; is it accurate.' The traditional reading accepted by the bhāṣya of Vyāsa and all other commentators is [['Sarvārthataikāgratayoh kṣayodayau cittasya samadhipariṇāmah'.\|'Sarvārthataikāgratayoḥ kṣayodayau cittasya samādhipariṇāmaḥ'.]] The [[Svami2314|Svami]][^2314] argues that the correct reading should be [['sarvarthatailarthayoh|'sarvārthataikārthayoḥ]] &c.' He states that the bhūmis should be six, the sixth being 'ekārtha' and bases his arguments on the combined explanations of Yoga sūtra I. [[2-4, 18 and III. 9-12.|2–4, 18 and III. 9–12.]] All that the present writer can say is that his arguments deserve serious consideration. The fact that even Vyāsa, the [[bhāsyakāra|bhāṣyakāra]] of Yogasūtra, would have to be held not to have seen the difficulties in the traditional reading of Y. S. III. 11 would have to be given due weight before any final conclusion can be arrived at. That sūtra states the goal of Yoga viz. the soul that is seer abides in its own form then [[(i.e.|(i. e.]] when the functions of the mind have been mastered), while in ordinary life the soul appears to assume the forms of the [[fluctua tions|fluctuations]] of the mind. The [[Vșttis B15|Vṛttis]][^2315] are five, some of which are

[^2315]: [[वृत्तयः पञ्चतथ्यः क्लिष्टाक्लिष्टाः। प्रमाणविपर्ययविकल्पनिद्रास्मृतयः। प्रत्यक्षा सुमानागमाः प्रमाणानि। ... अभाषमस्ययालम्बना वृत्तिनिद्रा। अनुभूतविषयासंप्रमोषः सुतिः। T. 1, 5-7 and 10-11, The 378 (hindrances or obstacles) are five, viz, अविद्या अस्मिता-राग-वेष-अभिनिवेशाः क्लेशाः (यो.. II. 3). The भाष्य on I. 818 'सेयं पशपर्वा भवत्यविद्या अविवास्मिता...निवेशाः क्लेशा इति। एत एव स्वसंज्ञाभिस्तमो मोहो महामोइस्लामिस्रोऽन्धतामिम इति अविद्या has live aspects (lit. joints) viz. अविधा etc. which are respectively called File etc; arrat explains all these, About अस्मिता he says योगिनामष्टस्वणिमादिकेष्वैश्वर्येवश्रेयः श्रेयाइद्धिरष्ट्राविधो मोहः पूर्व स्माजघन्यः। सचास्मितीच्यते।". The बुद्धचरित XII. 33 bas these.technical ideas: इत्यविद्या हि विद्वांसः पञ्चपर्क समीहते। तमो मोई महामोई तामिनद्वयमेव च. They are 80' called because they afflict mon plonged in Sansūra with several kinds of suffering. 'अविद्यादयः क्लेशाः विश्नन्ति खल्यमी पुरुष सांसारिक विविधदुःखमहारणविर पाचस्पति on यो..I. 24).|वृत्तयः पञ्चतय्यः क्लिष्टाक्लिष्टाः । प्रमाणविपर्ययविकल्पनिद्रास्मृतयः । प्रत्यक्षा[नुमानागमाः प्रमाणानि । ... अभावप्रत्ययालम्बना वृत्तिर्निद्रा । अनुभूतविषयासंप्रमोषः स्मृतिः । यो. सू. I. 5–7 and 10–11. The क्लेशs (hindrances or obstacles) are five, viz. अविद्या-अस्मिता-राग-द्वेष-अभिनिवेशाः क्लेशाः (यो. सू. II. 3). The भाष्य on I. 8 is 'सेयं पञ्चपर्वा भवत्यविद्या अविद्यास्मिता...निवेशाः क्लेशा इति । एत एव स्वसंज्ञाभिस्तमो मोहो महामोहोऽन्धतामिस्रोऽन्धतामिस्र इति' अविद्या has five aspects (lit. joints) viz. अविद्या etc. which are respectively called तम etc.; वाचस्पति explains all these. About अस्मिता he says "योगिनामष्टस्वणिमादिकेष्वैश्वर्येष्वश्रेयःसु श्रेयःश्रेयोबुद्धिरष्टविधो मोहः पूर्वस्माज्जघन्यः । स चास्मितेत्युच्यते ।". The बुद्धचरित XII. 33 has these technical ideas: इत्यविद्या हि विद्वांसः पञ्चपर्वां समीहते । तमो मोहं महामोहं तामिस्रद्वयमेव च ॥. They are so called because they afflict men plunged in Saṁsāra with several kinds of suffering. 'अविद्यादयः क्लेशाः क्लिश्नन्ति खल्वमी पुरुषं सांसारिकैर्विविधदुःखप्रहारैरिति' (वाचस्पति on यो. सू. I. 24).]]

[[P1410]][[affiliated|afflicted]] by hindrances called [[klesas|kleśas]] and the others are [[pot 80|not so]] hindered (those that are hindered are to be mastered or eliminated and the others are to be accepted). The five [[vpliis ,|vṛttis]] are pramūṇa [[(means of valid knowledge ), viparyaya (wrong Conception), rikalpa (fancy),|(means of valid knowledge), viparyaya (wrong conception), vikalpa (fancy),]] [[nidrū (sleep),|nidrā (sleep),]][^2316] [[smrti|smṛti]] (memory). [[Pramanas|Pramāṇas]] are three, [[pratyakṣa (perception), anumāna (inference), agama|pratyakṣa (perception), anumāna (inference), āgama]] (verbal testimony). The means for the suppression [[of: the vșttis are abhyūsu (practice) and rurūgya (passionlessness )|of the vṛttis are abhyāsa (practice) and vairāgya (passionlessness)]] (simultaneously carried on), the first being the effort to secure a calm flow of the mind free from [[vșttis,|vṛttis,]] continued for a long time uninterruptedly and earnestly and the latter (vairāgya) being the consciousness of mastery over (i. e. freedom from thirst for) seen objects (such as woman, food and drink, high [[position )|position)]] and objects promised by Revelation (such as heaven, disembodied existence [[&c.), 2317 Vairagya|&c.),]][^2317] 'Vairāgya is of two kinds apara (described in Y. S. I. 15 just above) and para (highest) described in Y. S. I. 16 and [[bhāsya theroon.|bhāṣya thereon.]] In the highest [[cairāgya|vairāgya]] the yogin (who has reached discrimination between the self and the [[quṇas,|guṇas,]] sattva &c.) is free from thirst not only for objects of sense, but also free from the [[gunas,|guṇas,]] attains [[& stage of updisturbed|a stage of undisturbed]] consciousness only and leads the yogin to reflect [[.I|'I]] have attained what was to be attained, the [[klesas (hindrancos|kleśas (hindrances]]

[^2316]: According to the [[Yogabbāsya|Yogabhāṣya]] on Y. S. [[I, 10|I. 10]] sleep is a special positive idea or experience [[(Pratyaya)|(pratyaya)]] and [[aot|not]] a mere absence of [[ady|any]] activities or fluctuations of the mind, since when a man rises from sleep he [[reilects 'I bave sept well, my miod is bappy|reflects 'I have slept well, my mind is happy]] and makes my intellect clear' and this reflection on waking would not be possible if there had been no experience (during sleep) of the cause of such a feeling. Just as in [[samadhi|samādhi]] one [[bas|has]] to master other ideas [[(viz,|viz.]] misconception, fancy [[&c.), 50 tbc|&c.), so the]] Yogi has to master sleep also as a [[bindrance|hindrance]] to the attainment of Samādhi.

The you on I. 16 remarks are aware that al प्रसादमात्रम् । ... ज्ञानस्थेष पराकाष्ठा वैराग्यम् । एतस्यैव हि नाम्तरीय कैवल्पमिति", In this 2nd kind of vairāgya there is nothiog bot an undisturbed and calm consciousoess or knowledge (unconnected with any object whatever) and Kæivalya (the goal of yoga) is inseparably connected with it.|अभ्यासवैराग्याभ्यां तन्निरोधः । तत्र स्थितौ यत्नोऽभ्यासः । स तु दीर्घकालनैरन्तर्यसत्कारासेवितो दृढभूमिः । दृष्टानुश्रविकविषयवितृष्णस्य वशीकारसंज्ञा वैराग्यम् । तत्परं पुरुषख्यातेर्गुणवैतृष्ण्यम् । यो. सू. I. 12–16. On sūtra I. 15 the bhāṣya says 'स्त्रियोऽन्नपानमैश्वर्यमिति दृष्टविषये विरक्तस्य स्वर्गवैदेह्यप्रकृतिलयत्वप्राप्तावानुश्रविकविषये वितृष्णस्य दिव्यादिव्यविषयसंप्रयोगेऽपि चित्तस्य विषयदोषदर्शिनः प्रसंख्यानबलादनाभोगात्मिका हेयोपादेयशून्या वशीकारसंज्ञा वैराग्यम् ।' ; वाचस्पति explains 'अनुश्रवो वेदस्ततोऽभिगताः आनुश्रविकाः स्वर्गादयः । ...... न तृष्णामात्रं वैराग्यं, अपि तु दिव्यादिव्यविषयसंप्रयोगेऽपि चित्तस्यानाभोगात्मिका ।' For the words दृष्ट and आनुश्रविक, compare सां. का. 2 दृष्टवदानुश्रविकः स ह्यविशुद्धिक्षयातिशययुक्तः । तद्विपरीतः श्रेयान् व्यक्ताव्यक्तज्ञविज्ञानात् ॥. The भाष्य on I. 16 remarks 'तद्द्वयं वैराग्यम् । तत्र यदुत्तरं तज्ज्ञानप्रसादमात्रम् । ... ज्ञानस्यैव पराकाष्ठा वैराग्यम् । एतस्यैव हि नान्तरीयकं कैवल्यमिति ।'. In this 2nd kind of vairāgya there is nothing but an undisturbed and calm consciousness or knowledge (unconnected with any object whatever) and Kaivalya (the goal of yoga) is inseparably connected with it.]]

[[P1411]][[a vidya &o.)|avidyā &c.)]] that have to be destroyed have been destroyed, the close-knit succession of births and deaths has been [[out|cut]] off'. The Bhāṣya remarks 'the highest limit of consciousness [[(knowledge )|(knowledge)]] is Vairāgya and Kaivalya is inseparably connected with it.' [[Vāoaspati|Vācaspati]] states that this last is called [[' Dharmamegha-samadhi'|'Dharmamegha-samādhi']] (mentioned in Y. S. IV. 29). Sūtras 17 and 18 of first pāda deal respectively with [[samprajñāta samadhi|samprajñāta-samādhi]] (conscious concentration) or [[sālambabasamādhi|sālambanasamādhi]] and [[asamprajñāta-samādbi|asamprajñāta-samādhi]] (concentration in which there is no [[con sciousness|consciousness]] of objects, gross or subtle). The first is of four kinds (or of four stages) viz. savitarka (deliberation over or [[realiza tion|realization]] of a gross object like [[Sālagrāma|Śālagrāma]] or the image of a god with four arms and the like), savicāra [[( where|(where]] there is reflection over [[&|a]] subtle object such as the tan-mātrās), sānanda where the reflection is on the mind which is full of sattva (that is a [[samadhi of joy )|samādhi of joy)]] and sāsmitarūpa (i. e. where there is only a sense of personality in which the knower himself is the [[objeot|object]] of [[perception). 2318|perception).]][^2318] These four kinds lead on to the [[asamprajñātam|asamprajñāta-]]samādhi, which results when all [[vșttis|vṛttis]] have disappeared, when there is persistent exercise of this state and the mind consists of [[subliminal|sub-liminal]] impressions only. Sūtras 19-51 (to the end of the first pāda) deal with the different kinds of [[samadhi,|samādhi,]] the different ways of attainment, the position of [[Iśvara|Īśvara]] in the Yoga system, the nine obstacles (antarāya) that cause distraction of the mind of the person who [[practices|practises]] Yoga and the companions of these

The भाष्य on I. 18 states तदग्यास पूर्वकं हि चित्तं निरालम्बनमभाषप्रातमिव भवतीत्येष निजः समाधि:. The भाग्य on यो.छ. 1. 2 states 'faffst: HATTE: 1 a Feraraitua chata: 1 far: \# miweraniugais. It is somewhat surprising that one kind of samadhi is characterized by .asmitā'. Probably ascitā bere means no more than tbe conciousness 'I am' (i. e. of personality).
